Part 2: Learning Objectives By the end of this part of the module students will be able to: 1. Classify and describe different ideological perspectives towards sexuality education in the schools. 2. Identify and describe Canadian's attitudes towards sexuality education in the schools. 3. Assess adolescent's needs with respect to sexuality education. 4. Identify the key ingredients of effective adolescent sexuality education. Topic Overview Introduction As we saw in Module 1, and throughout the course so far, the social context of human sexuality in Western culture can be examined in terms of the struggle between the Restrictive and Permissive sexual ideologies. Although Western society appears, over time, to be tilting towards Permissive sexual ideology, there are many sexuality issues upon which Canadian society remains divided. The issue of sexuality education in the schools is also a good case study for examining societal attitudes towards sexuality. \"Sex education is potentially a vehicle for social engineering par excellence, be it progressive or traditional\". \"...educators in the field of sexuality inevitably do their work under some assumptions about its purpose and value - assumptions that include a particular vision of the place of sexuality in social behaviour in general and the needs of teenagers in particular\" . In Module 4, we became familiar with the concept of socialization (The ways in which society conveys to the individual its expectations for his or her behaviour). To a great extent, our sexuality related attitudes and behaviours are the result of the socialization process that occurs as we are growing up. Along with families, friends, religious institutions, and the media, schools are among the most powerful socialization agents in our society. It's not surprising then, that opinions about what should be taught in school-based sexuality education will be a reflection of different ideological perspectives towards sexuality generally. Key Point Schools are one of the primary mechanisms for the socialization of youth in our society and, as a result, sexuality education in the schools is often perceived as a powerful force in shaping the sexuality attitudes and values of youth. Ideology and Sexuality Education: Different Perspectives Restrictive Sexual Ideology: During the 1970's and 1980's, proponents of Restrictive sexual ideology argued that sexuality education did not belong in the schools. In this view, education about sexuality should be left exclusively to parents and religious institutions. From this perspective, sexuality education in the schools would provoke increased sexual curiosity and behaviour among youth. As it became clear, particularly in the 1990's, that schools would play some role in educating youth about sexuality, proponents of Restrictive sexual ideology tended to modify their position to argue that school-based programs should be focused on the primary objective of teaching youth that sexual activity outside of marriage is immoral, psychologically harmful, and unhealthy. These so-called \"abstinence-only\" programs have been far more popular in the United States than in Canada. Indeed, the U.S. government actively funds and encourages abstinence programs. Legislation signed into law by U.S. President Bill Clinton in 1996 included a provision that in order to receive federal funding, sexuality education programs must teach \"...that a mutually monogamous relationship in the context of marriage is the expected standard of human sexual activity\" and \"...that sexual activity outside the context of marriage is likely to have harmful psychological and physical effects\" (cited in Daley, 1997, ). Permissive Sexual Ideology: During the 1970's and 1980's, proponents of Permissive sexual ideology argued that sexuality education did belong in the schools. From this perspective, sexuality education in the schools would help to reduce sexuality related social problems such as teen pregnancy and HIV STI. In this view, knowledge was preferable to ignorance and youth should be taught that sexuality is a natural and positive part of life. Students would be encouraged to clarify their own values about the appropriateness of premarital sex and they would also be informed about birth control methods and ways to reduce the risks of sexual activity (e.g., condom use). Although many school-based sexuality education programs in Canada today make a point of stressing the advantages of abstinence, most programs, particularly in non-religious public schools, do acknowledge the importance of dual protection against pregnancy and HIV STD for sexually active youth. A Democratic Approach to Sexuality Education: Is it possible for sexuality education in the schools to avoid indoctrinating youth with a particular sexual ideology? In one respect, it seems clear that as Canadian society has, in some respects, adopted a more Permissive sexual ideology, these shifts in social attitudes have been reflected in our approaches to teaching youth about sexuality. For example, teaching that using condoms is a wise choice for sexually active youth or including the sexual health education needs of LGBTQI2S students in school curricula can be seen as examples of sexuality education being informed by Permissive sexual ideology. On the other hand, many sexuality educators and policy makers, particularly in Canada, have suggested a philosophical approach to sexuality education that is based, first and foremost, not in either Restrictive or Permissive sexual ideology, but rather on the basic principles of a democratic society. As noted above, the U.S. government unambiguously sides with Restrictive sexual ideology by funding and promoting abstinence-only sex education. Although in Canada, education is a provincial responsibility, the Canadian federal government has played a role in the past in sexuality education. A notable example of this was the Public Health Agency of Canada's Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education which took a quite different approach than the stance taken by the U.S. government. Consider the following statements from those Canadian guidelines. The Guidelines stateed that effective sexual health education: \"is structured so that changes in behaviour and confidence is developed as a result of nonjudgemental and informed decision-making\" (). \"should be provided in an age-appropriate, culturally sensitive manner that is respectful of individual sexual diversity, abilities and choices\" (). In sum, it would appear that, in contrast to U.S. policy, Canadian approaches to sexuality education reflect basic democratic principles such as the right to self-determination and the right to receive information that enables people to make their own choices. For a more modern version of these principles see the Core Principles of Comprehensive Sexual Health Education from the 2019 edition of the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education: Before examining Canadian's attitudes towards sexuality education in schools, lets take a very quick look at the history of school-based sexuality education in Canada and briefly evaluate the quality of current sex education programs. A Brief History of School-based Sexuality Education in Canada Up until the 1970's, there were few debates about sexuality education in the schools, largely because few schools had sexuality education programs. Students sometimes received limited information about reproduction and sexually transmitted diseases through lectures on \"personal hygiene\", or through biology and physical education classes. Beginning in the 1970's, concern about teenage pregnancies combined with increasing societal openness about sexuality led to the gradual implementation of sexuality education programs in Canadian schools. In the 1980's and 1990's, alarm over HIV AIDS, high rates of other sexually transmitted infections in youth, as well as a growing recognition that most Canadian youth were becoming sexually active in their teens, prompted most secondary schools in Canada to introduce some form of sexuality education. By the mid-1990's, every province and territory in Canada either mandated or strongly recommended, through provincial departments of education or health, that some form of sexuality education be taught in the schools . More recently, the province of Quebec has removed sexual health education from its formal curriculum although the Quebec Ministry of Education recommends that teachers find ways to integrate sexual health issues into classroom teaching. Key Point Every province and territory in Canada either mandates or strongly recommends that some form of sexuality education be taught in the schools. The Current Status of Sexuality Education in Canadian Schools What can we say about the current status of sexuality education in Canadian schools? Although it would be accurate to say that most Canadian youth receive some form of sexuality education before they leave high school, it is also true that few Canadian youth receive sexuality education that provides them with the information and skills necessary to protect and enhance their sexual health. Key Point Although most Canadian youth receive some form of sexuality education before leaving high school, few of these programs provide the necessary information, motivation, and behavioural skills that youth need to protect and enhance their sexual health. The quality of sexuality education in Canadian schools varies from school to school, from school board to school board and from province to province. Thus, it is difficult to generalize about the quality of sexuality education in Canadian schools. However, a number of observations can be made that suggest that there are many weaknesses in contemporary sexuality education. 1. According to data from the Canadian Community Health Survey (Rotermann, 2012; SIECCAN, 2020), about 9 of youth have intercourse before age 15 and another 26 have intercourse at 15 or 16. Yet, many, if not most Canadian youth do not receive detailed education on birth control and condom use until the later grades of high school. In other words, by the time they receive sexuality education, many youth have already become sexually active. 2. Sexual health education programs for adolescents tend to focus primarily on the prevention of sexual problems such as STIs but pay little attention to the enhancement of sexual health and well-being (e.g., healthy, mutually satisfying and pleasurable relationships). 3. Many sexuality education programs in Canadian schools focus on providing basic factual information on the biological aspects of human sexuality. As we will see below, simply providing youth with information on the biological aspects of human sexuality is unlikely, by itself, to result in youth adopting behavioural practices to protect and enhance their sexual health. 4. Very few school teachers have been given specific training on how to provide effective sexuality education programs. 5. Many school principles and administrators believe that providing sexuality education in the schools is highly controversial. They are, therefore, often reluctant to have topics such as sexual orientation and condom use included in the curriculum out of fear that many parents will object to these topics being taught. Catholic schools, in particular, are often reluctant to teach these topics. Canadian Attitudes Towards Sexuality Education As noted above, there is a common perception that sexuality education in the schools is a controversial topic and that many parents strongly object to the idea that their children will be taught about topics such as sexual orientation and condom use. To what extent is this true? Some parents do object to these topics being taught because they do not want the schools to contradict the religious or ethnocultural values related to sexuality that they are trying to pass on to their children. However, it is also true that most parents want their children to receive sexuality education in the schools. Key Point A strong majority of parents approve of sexuality education in the schools. Not only do most Canadian parents want sexuality education taught in the schools, parents also want these programs to teach a broad range of topics. Surveys of parents in Canada have found that most parents approve of the teaching of a wide range of topics including healthy relationships, sexual pleasure, masturbation, methods of STI prevention, birth control, gender identity, and sexual orientation (McKay ., 2014; SIECCAN, 2020; Wood ., 2021). Most Canadian parents appear to recognize the importance of providing youth with broadly-based sexuality education that addresses the realities of adolescent sexuality and sexual behaviour. Not surprisingly, surveys of Canadian youth clearly show that they want relevant sexual health education provided in their schools . However, these same studies also show that few students are satisfied with the sexual health education they are currently receiving . Here is a recent assessment from youth in Canada on their sexual health education experiences and needs: Working Towards Effective Sexuality Education in the Schools In your required reading for this module you will learn about the characteristics of effective sexuality education programs. Among the most important characteristics of effective programs is that they use theoretical models of behaviour change to create and teach the program. Key Point Effective sexuality education programs are informed by theoretical models of behaviour change. The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education demonstrate the Information, Motivation, Behavioural Skills theoretical model and we will use the IMB model here to illustrate the principles of effective sexuality education. For more information on the key ingredients of effective school-based sexual health education programs see the SIECCAN document Questions and Answers: Sexual Health Education in Schools and Other Settings. (Both of these document are available online at the SIECCAN website Traditionally, and in many cases today, sexuality education programs focus on providing students with factual information. For example, students might learn that condoms reduce the risk of HIV STI infection or that not having intercourse means that you don't have to worry about pregnancy. But does simply learning this kind of factual information translate into healthy behaviour? Research has consistently shown that learning factual information is not enough. Students must also be motivated to use the information they have learned and they must also learn the actual skills to incorporate the information into their behaviour. Let's look at two examples (obtaining discussing partner consent to sexual activity, using condoms, dental dams, or other barriers to prevent STI transmission) of how the IMB model can be used to formulate effective sexuality education for adolescents. Both of these examples are excerpted from the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education . It is important to have a historical perspective, as past decisions have shaped (and constrained) current decisions about municipalities. The module introduced charter cities, considered whether there is a right size for municipalities, and showed that there is consistency in issues that challenge municipalities from the past and today, like housing and infrastructure. We considered the role of the mayor and councillors and the decision-making structure of council and different types of committees. Complex Environment Municipalities operate in complex environments. The structure of municipalities can be complicated with regional, two-tier structures as well as municipal and provincial special purpose bodies. Elected representatives are not associated with political parties, which can make decision-making more robust, but at the same time more complicated and more difficult to predict. Downloading has added complexity associated with provincial program requirements and funding and municipal funding, particularly grants, and is subject to change. Relationships Municipalities have formal and informal relationships with the public and many interests, stakeholders, and levels of government. As the level of government closest to the people, these relationships bring high expectations regarding efficiency, effectiveness, representation, and engagement in decision- making processes. Municipalities face significant limitations in a formal relationship with the federal government, even though federal policies frequently have direct implications for municipalities. Municipal relationships with provinces are politically unstable and change happens in unanticipated forms. Relationships with Indigenous governments and communities have become increasingly important to municipalities. Capacity Municipalities have limited financial and jurisdictional capacity to address the increasingly complicated local challenges and issues. The course looked at a number of ways that the lack of financial capacity can be addressed, but action has been limited. Lack of jurisdictional capacity was a recurring theme throughout the course. Apr 06 08:50:48 raspberrypi node[8770]: 8:50:48 AM [express] GET /api/cat-wash/progress 304 in 1ms :: {"active":true,"fileId":231,"fileName":"CPPA122, Module 12 - Introduction.pdf","chunkIndex":8,"totalChunks":13,"chunkText":"However, more frequently, provincial decisions overlook municipal and public opposition to the changes. Relationships Municipalities have formal and informal relationships with the public and many interests, stakeholders, and levels of government. As the level of government closest to the people, these relationships bring high expectations regarding efficiency, effectiveness, representation, and engagement in decision- making processes. Municipalities face significant limitations in a formal relationship with the federal government, even though federal policies frequently have direct implications for municipalities. Municipal relationships with provinces are politically unstable and change happens in unanticipated forms. Relationships with Indigenous governments and communities have become increasingly important to municipalities. Capacity Municipalities have limited financial and jurisdictional capacity to address the increasingly complicated local challenges and issues. The course looked at a number of ways that the lack of financial capacity can be addressed, but action has been limited. Lack of jurisdictional capacity was a recurring theme throughout the course. Relationships Municipalities have formal and informal relationships with the public and many interests, stakeholders, and levels of government. As the level of government closest to the people, these relationships bring high expectations regarding efficiency, effectiveness, representation, and engagement in decision- making processes. Municipalities face significant limitations in a formal relationship with the federal government, even though federal policies frequently have direct implications for municipalities. Municipal relationships with provinces are politically unstable and change happens in unanticipated forms.
